On Difficulty of Breathing

De difficultate respirationis · Kühn VII 753-960

The first complete English translation we know of. No complete translation in any modern language. One paragraph (Kühn VII 781–782) is in English in A. A. Long 2021. K7.825-826K. VII 825–826, ch. 2.1 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 1/1 (c) pass 2/2 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἔστι μὲν οὖν ἡ ἐξήγησις, ὥς πού τις τῶν παλαιῶν εἶπεν, ἀσαφοῦς ἑρμηνείας ἐξάπλωσις· ἡμεῖς δ’ οὐ τοῦτο μόνον ἐοίκαμεν δράσειν, ἀλλ’ ὅτι καὶ ἀληθῶς ἔχει πάντα τὰ περὶ δυσπνοίας ὑπ’ 826 αὐτοῦ γεγραμμένα, μαρτυρήσειν, οὐ μὴν ἀποδείξειν οὐδὲν αὐτῶν ἐν τῷδε τῷ γράμματι, ἀλλ’ εἰς τὸ προηγούμενον τούτου βιβλίον ἀναπέμψειν, ἐν ᾧ χωρὶς ἀποδείξεως οὐδὲν εἴρηται. καὶ ἵνα γε μὴ δυσωποῖτό τις ὑπὸ τῆς Ἱπποκράτους δόξης, καὶ θᾶττον ἤδη τοῖς λεγομένοις πιστεύοι πρὶν ὑπὸ τῶν ἀποδείξεων ἀναγκασθῆναι, τὸν πρῶτον λόγον ἰδίᾳ τε καὶ καθ’ ἑαυτὸν ἐγράψαμεν· ἐπεὶ δ’ ἐκεῖνος ἐτελειώθη μετὰ τῶν προσηκόντων συλλογισμῶν, ἐν τῷδε τῷ γράμματι τὸν εὑρετήν τε καὶ οἷον πατέρα τῶν δογμάτων τιμήσομεν. αὕτη καὶ δικαία τέ ἐστιν ἅμα καὶ πρέπουσα τοῖς ὁτιοῦν ἀγαθὸν ἐξευροῦσι τιμὴ, μήτε κατακεκρύφθαι τὰ εὑρέματα αὐτῶν μήτ’ ἀγνοεῖσθαι τοῖς ἀνθρώποις, ἀλλ’ αὐτά τε φαίνεσθαι τήν θ’ εὑροῦσαν αὐτὰ μέθοδον γιγνώσκεσθαι. οὕτως γὰρ αὐτοῖς καὶ τὸ θαυμάζεσθαι κατὰ τὴν ἀξίαν ὑπάρξει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Exegesis, then, as one of the ancients somewhere said, is the unfolding of an unclear expression; but we seem likely to do not only this, but also to bear witness that everything written by 826 him about dyspnoea holds true, yet not to demonstrate any of it in this treatise, but to refer back to the book preceding this one, in which nothing has been said without demonstration. And so that no one should be overawed by the reputation of Hippocrates and so believe what is said too soon, before being compelled by the demonstrations, we wrote the first discourse separately and on its own; but since that one was brought to completion with the appropriate syllogisms, in this treatise we shall honour the discoverer and, as it were, father of the doctrines. This is the honour that is both just and at the same time fitting for those who have discovered any good thing whatever: that their discoveries be neither hidden away nor unknown to men, but that the discoveries themselves be made manifest and the method that discovered them be known. For in this way they will also be admired in accordance with their worth.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δυσωποῖτο, ἐοίκαμεν δράσειν

Now exegesis is, as one of the ancients somewhere said, the unfolding of an obscure expression; but we, it seems, shall do not only this, but shall also bear witness that everything written by 826 him about difficulty of breathing is true. We shall not, however, demonstrate any of it in this treatise, but shall refer back to the book preceding this one, in which nothing has been said without demonstration. And so that no one should be overawed by the reputation of Hippocrates and so trust what is said too readily, before being compelled by the demonstrations, we wrote the first book separately and on its own. But since that book has been completed with the appropriate syllogisms, in this treatise we shall honour the discoverer and, as it were, father of the doctrines. This honour is at once just and fitting for those who have discovered any good thing whatever: that their discoveries be neither hidden away nor unknown to men, but that the discoveries themselves be made manifest and the method that discovered them be recognised. For in this way they will also come to be admired according to their worth.

Greek (Kühn)

Πρῶτον οὖν, ὅπερ ἀεὶ διαφυλάττειν ἀξιοῦμεν οἷς ἂν ἀλήθεια σπουδάζηται, τὸ μηδὲν ἀποκρύπτεσθαι τῶν ἐναργῶς φαινομένων, μηδ’ ἂν ὅτι μάλιστα τὸν τῆς αἰτίας 827 λογισμὸν ἀπορώτατον ἔχῃ, τοῦτ’ ἐπαινεῖν Ἱπποκράτην ἄξιον ἔν τε τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασι καὶ τοῖς περὶ δυσπνοίας λόγοις οὐχ ἥκιστα. τὸ γὰρ ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα πνεῦμα μυρίοις μὲν τῶν παραφρονούντων ὁσημέραι φαίνεται συμπίπτειν, ὅσοι δ’ οὐ δύνανται τῶν σοφιστῶν εἰπεῖν αὐτοῦ τὴν αἰτίαν, οὗτοι δ’ εἰσὶν, ὡς ἔμπροσθεν ἐδείξαμεν, οἱ τὴν ἀναπνοὴν ἀπροαίρετόν τε καὶ φυσικὸν ἔργον ὑπειληφότες, οὐδὲ γίγνεσθαί φασιν οὐδέποτε τοιοῦτον εἶδος δυσπνοίας, ἐξ ὧν περὶ τῆς αἰτίας ἀποροῦσιν, ἐκ τούτων τὴν οὐσίαν τοῦ πράγματος ἀνατρέπειν τολμῶντες, δέον οὐ ταύτην, ἀλλὰ τὴν αὑτῶν μοχθηρὰν ὑπόληψιν ἀνατρέψαντας αὐτὰ τὰ συμπίπτοντα τοῖς νοσοῦσι παραφυλάττειν, ὡς Ἱπποκράτης ἐποίει. τουτωῒ γάρ, φησι, πνεῦμα διὰ τέλεος, ὥσπερ ἀνακαλουμένῳ, ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα. τουτωῒ, Φιλίσκῳ δηλονότι, τῷ πρώτῳ γεγραμμένῳ μετὰ τὰς τρεῖς καταστάσεις ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν, ἆρ’ οὖν τούτῳ μόνῳ τὸ πνεῦμα μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν, ἢ καὶ ἄλλοις τισὶν Ἱπποκράτης ἐθεάσατο γιγνόμενον;

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνακαλουμένῳ

First, then, that which we deem it right always to preserve for those by whom truth is pursued, namely to conceal none of the things that evidently appear, not even if the 827 reasoning concerning its cause is in the highest degree perplexing—for this it is fitting to praise Hippocrates both in all other matters and not least in his discussions on difficulty of breathing. For rare and large breath is seen every day to befall countless numbers of those who are delirious; but those among the sophists who are unable to state its cause—and these are, as we showed before, those who have supposed respiration to be an involuntary and natural function—say that such a form of difficulty of breathing never even occurs at all, daring, on the basis of those matters about which they are at a loss concerning the cause, to overturn the very existence of the thing, when they ought not to overturn this, but their own faulty supposition, and to observe closely the very things that befall the sick, as Hippocrates did. For in this man, he says, the breath throughout was, as of one recollecting himself, rare and large. In this man—that is, in Philiscus, the first written up after the three constitutions in the first book of the Epidemics. Was it, then, in this man alone that Hippocrates saw the breath occurring large and rare, or also in certain others?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνακαλουμένῳ, ἀπροαίρετόν

First, then, there is that which we hold ought always to be preserved by those who are in earnest about truth, namely that none of the things which evidently appear be concealed, not even if the reasoning about the 827 cause is in the highest degree perplexing. For this it is right to praise Hippocrates, both in all his other writings and not least in his discussions of difficulty of breathing. For sparse and large breath is seen every day to occur in countless persons who are deranged; but those of the sophists who are unable to state its cause (and these are, as we showed earlier, the ones who have supposed breathing to be an unwilled and natural function) say that such a kind of difficulty of breathing never even occurs at all. On the basis of the things about whose cause they are at a loss, they dare to overturn the reality of the thing itself, when they ought to have overturned not this but their own faulty supposition, and to watch carefully the very things that befall the sick, as Hippocrates did. 'For this man,' he says, 'breath throughout, as of one recollecting, sparse and large.' 'This man,' that is, Philiscus, the first patient written up after the three constitutions in the first book of the Epidemics. Was it, then, in this man alone that the breath was large and sparse, or did Hippocrates observe it occurring in certain others as well?

Greek (Kühn)

οὐ τούτῳ μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἄλλοις τε πολλοῖς καὶ τῷ μετὰ ταῦτα γεγραμμένῳ, 828 Σιληνῷ τοὔνομα. διελθὼν γὰρ καὶ περὶ τούτου πάνθ’ ὅσα συνέπεσεν αὐτῷ νοσοῦντι, κατ’ αὐτὴν τοῦ λόγου τὴν τελευτὴν ταῦτα γράφει. ἐξ ἀρχῆς τούτῳ καὶ διὰ τέλεος πνεῦμα ἀραιὸν, μέγα. αὖθις δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς ἀῤῥώστοις, τὴν Δρομάδεώ, φησι, γυναῖκα τεκοῦσαν, καὶ τῶν ἄλλων γενομένων πάντων κατὰ λόγον, δευτεραίην ἐοῦσαν ῥῖγος ἔλαβεν. εἶτα μικρὸν προελθών φησι· πνεῦμα ἀραιὸν, μέγα. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν εὐθὺς ἐν τῇ πρώτῃ τῶν ἡμερῶν ἔγραψεν οὕτως, αὖθις δὲ περὶ τῆς στ΄ διαλεγόμενος ὡδὶ γράφει· ἕκτῃ πρωῒ ἐπεῤῥίγωσε, ταχὺ διεθερμάνθη, ἵδρωσεν διόλου. ἄκρεα ψυχρά. παρέκρουσε. πνεῦμα ἀραιὸν, μέγα. ἐν μὲν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἑκκαίδεκα τοὺς πάντας ἀῤῥώστους Ἱπποκράτης γράψας, τετράκις φαίνεται μνημονεύων ἀραιοῦ καὶ μεγάλου πνεύματος. οἱ γενναῖοι δ’ ἡμῖν οὗτοι σοφισταὶ τὴν ἀρχὴν οὐδὲ ἑωρακέναι φασὶν οὐδένα μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἀναπνέοντα. (ἢ) ἀλλ’ ἴσως μὲν τοῦτο αὐτοῖς πιστευτέον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: (ἢ), κατὰ λόγον

Not in this man only, but also in many others and in the one described after these, 828 Silenus by name. For having gone through, in his case too, everything that befell him while he was ill, at the very end of the account he writes these words: "In this man from the beginning and throughout, breathing rare, large." And again, among the patients that follow, "The wife of Dromeades," he says, "having given birth, and all the other things having gone according to reason, being on the second day, a rigor seized her." Then, proceeding a little further, he says: "breathing rare, large." This, then, he wrote in this way straightaway for the first of the days; and again, when discussing the sixth, he writes as follows: "On the sixth, early in the morning, she had a rigor, quickly became thoroughly warm, sweated all over. Extremities cold. She was delirious. Breathing rare, large." In the first book of the Epidemics, then, Hippocrates, having described sixteen patients in all, is seen making mention of rare and large breathing four times. But these noble sophists of ours say that they have never seen anyone at all breathing large and rare. (ἢ) But perhaps on this point they are to be believed.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: (ἢ), ἐπεῤῥίγωσε, παρέκρουσε

Not to him alone, but also to many others, and in particular to the one written up after these, 828 Silenus by name. For having gone through, in his case too, everything that befell him while he was ill, at the very end of the account he writes this: 'From the beginning and throughout, this man had breathing sparse, large.' Again, among the patients that follow, 'The wife of Dromeades,' he says, 'having given birth, and everything else having gone as it should, on the second day was seized by rigor.' Then, going on a little further, he says: 'Breathing sparse, large.' This, then, he wrote thus right on the first of the days; and again, discussing the sixth, he writes as follows: 'On the sixth, early in the morning, she had a renewed rigor; quickly became warm throughout; sweated all over. Extremities cold. She wandered in her mind. Breathing sparse, large.' In the first book of the Epidemics Hippocrates, having written up sixteen patients in all, is seen to mention sparse and large breathing four times. Yet these noble sophists of ours say that they have never at all seen anyone breathing large and sparse. (Or) but perhaps in this they are to be believed.

Greek (Kühn)

οὐ γὰρ ἑωράκασιν οὔτε ταύτης οὔτ’ ἄλλης δυσπνοίας διαφορὰν οὐδεμίαν, ὅτι μηδὲ 829 τοὺς ἀῤῥωστοῦντας αὐτοὺς ὁρῶσιν, ἀλλ’ ἔνδον καθέζονται, μοχθηρὰ σοφίσματα συντιθέντες. Ἆρ’ οὖν ἔτι καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἀῤῥώστων μνημονευτέον ἡμῖν ἐστιν, οὓς ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις βιβλίοις τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἔγραψεν μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἀναπνέοντας; ἢ πρὸς μὲν τὴν τοῦ νῦν προκειμένου πίστιν ἀρκεῖ μὲν ταῦτα; φαίνεται γὰρ ὁ ὑπὸ πάντων ἰατρῶν Ἱπποκράτης ὡμολογημένος εἰς τὴν τῶν συμβαινόντων τοῖς νοσοῦσι τήρησιν ἐπιμελέστατος γεγονέναι, πολλοὺς ἑωρακὼς οὕτως ἀναπνέοντας. ἐν δὲ τῷ μετὰ ταῦτα λόγῳ καὶ ἄλλων τινῶν ἀῤῥώστων ἀναγκαῖον ἡμῖν ἐστι μνημονεῦσαι μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἀναπνευσάντων, οὓς ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς βιβλίοις ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης· ἀλλ’ οὐκ εἰς πίστιν ἔσται τοῦ νῦν προκειμένου, τοῦτο μὲν γὰρ ἤδη δῆλον, ἀλλ’ εἰς ἑτέρων τινῶν ἐν τῷ κατ’ αὐτοὺς λόγῳ περιεχομένων ζητημάτων ἐξήγησιν, ἐν παρέργῳ δὲ καὶ ἡ πίστις τοῦ πολλοὺς οὕτως ἀναπνεῖν αὐξηθήσεται. τίνος οὖν διαθέσεως ἡ τοιαύτη δύσπνοια γνωριστική; οὐδεμιᾶς ἄλλης, ὅτι μὴ παραφροσύνης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For they have seen no difference either of this or of any other dyspnoea, since they do not even 829 see the sick themselves, but sit indoors, putting together wretched sophisms. Must we, then, still mention also the other patients whom he recorded in the other books of the Epidemics as breathing great and rare? Or do these suffice for the confirmation of what is now proposed? For Hippocrates, who is acknowledged by all physicians to have been most careful in the observation of what befalls the sick, evidently saw many breathing in this way. But in the discussion that follows this it will be necessary for us to mention certain other patients too who breathed great and rare, whom Hippocrates recorded in the subsequent books; yet this will not be for the confirmation of what is now proposed—for that is already clear—but for the explanation of certain other questions contained in the account concerning them, and incidentally the confirmation that many breathe in this way will also be increased. Of what condition, then, is such a dyspnoea diagnostic? Of no other than delirium.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀρκεῖ μὲν (the second μέν, after πρὸς μὲν, has no answering δέ), ἐν τῷ κατ’ αὐτοὺς λόγῳ, παραφροσύνης

For they have not seen any distinction either of this or of any other difficulty of breathing, since they do not even 829 look at the sick themselves, but sit indoors, putting together wicked sophisms. Must we, then, go on to mention also the other patients whom he recorded in the other books of the Epidemics as breathing large and sparse? Or are these sufficient for the proof of what is now under discussion? For Hippocrates, who is acknowledged by all physicians to have been most diligent in the observation of what befalls the sick, evidently saw many breathing in this way. In the following book, however, we shall need to mention also certain other patients who breathed large and sparse, whom Hippocrates recorded in the subsequent books. That will not be for the proof of what is now under discussion, for this is already clear, but for the explanation of certain other questions contained in the account of them; and incidentally the proof that many breathe in this way will also be strengthened. Of what condition, then, is such difficulty of breathing indicative? Of none other than derangement.

Greek (Kühn)

﻿ 830 διὰ τίνα τὴν αἰτίαν, ἐν μὲν τῷ προτέρῳ λόγῳ σαφῶς δέδεικται, ὑπομνήσεως δ’ ἕνεκα καὶ νῦν εἰρήσεται διὰ βραχέων. τῷ γὰρ ὄντι παραφροσύνης ἴδιον σύμπτωμα μεγάλην τε καὶ ἀραιὰν, ὅπερ ἐστὶ διὰ πολλοῦ τοῦ χρόνου, τὴν ἀναπνοὴν ποιεῖσθαι. τί γὰρ ἔδει πλέον εἰσπνεῖν, μὴ δεόμενον πλέονος; ἢ τί διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου, παρὸν δι’ ἐλάττονος; ὅτι δ’ οὐ δέονται πλέονος ἀέρος οἱ μέγα τε καὶ ἀραιὸν εἰσπνέοντες, ἡ ἀραιότης κατηγορεῖ. πάντως γὰρ ἂν ἠπείγοντο συνεχέστερον εἰσπνεῖν, εἴπερ ἐδέοντο πλέονος· τὸ δ’ ἡσυχάζειν εἰς μακρὸν σημεῖόν ἐστι τοῦ μὴ δεῖσθαι πολλοῦ. ἀλλ’ ἔοικεν ἄμφω ταῦτα οἷον ἐπιλανθανομένου τῆς ἐνεργείας εἶναι παθήματα. τὸ γὰρ ἄμετρον μὲν ποιήσασθαι τὴν ὁλκὴν τοῦ πνεύματος, ἄμετρον δὲ τὴν ἡσυχίαν, οὔθ’ ὅπου καταπαῦσαι χρὴ τὴν ἐνέργειαν εἰδότος ἐστὶν οὔθ’ ὅτε πάλιν ἄρξασθαι, ἀλλ’ ἐν ἀμφοτέροις τὸ μέτρον ὑπερβαίνοντος ἀγνοίᾳ τοῦ προσήκοντος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

830 For what cause has been clearly demonstrated in the preceding discourse, but for the sake of reminder it will be stated now too in brief. For in truth it is a symptom peculiar to delirium to make the respiration great and rare, that is, at a long interval of time. For why should one breathe in more, when not in need of more? Or why at a long interval, when it is possible at a shorter one? And that those who breathe in great and rare do not need more air, the rarity testifies. For they would certainly be pressed to breathe in more continuously, if indeed they needed more; but remaining at rest for a long time is a sign of not needing much. But both of these seem to be affections, as it were, of one who is forgetting the activity. For to make the drawing of the breath immoderate, and the rest immoderate, belongs to one who knows neither where he ought to stop the activity nor when to begin it again, but who in both exceeds the measure through ignorance of what is fitting.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅπου

830 For what reason, has been clearly shown in the previous book; but for the sake of reminder it will be stated now too, briefly. For in truth it is a symptom peculiar to derangement to make the breathing large and sparse, that is, at long intervals of time. For why should one breathe in more when one does not need more? Or why at long intervals of time, when it is possible at shorter ones? That those who breathe in large and sparse do not need more air, the infrequency itself proves. For they would certainly hasten to breathe in more continuously if they needed more; but remaining at rest for a long time is a sign of not needing much. Rather, both of these seem to be affections of one who, as it were, forgets the activity. For to make the drawing of the breath immoderate, and the rest immoderate, belongs to one who knows neither at what point he must stop the activity nor when he must begin it again, but who in both exceeds the measure through ignorance of what is fitting.

K7.830-831K. VII 830–831, ch. 2.3 difficulty mid (2.35/100; thin 3, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ὡς οὖν ἀμίδα πολλάκις αἰτήσαντες, ἵν’ οὐρήσωσιν, οἱ παραφρονοῦντες ἐπελάθοντο ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ, εἶτ’ οὐκ ἂν οὐρήσαιεν, εἰ μὴ 831 ὑπομνήσει τις, οὕτως ἐπειδὴ καὶ τὸ ἀναπνεῖν ψυχικόν ἐστιν ἔργον, βέβλαπται δ’ αὐτοῖς ὁ λογισμὸς, ἐπιλησμονέστεροι περί τε τὰς ἐνεργείας αὐτὰς καὶ περὶ τὰ διαλείμματα αὐτῶν γίγνονται. ἀμφότερα οὖν ἐν πολλῷ χρόνῳ ποιοῦντες, καὶ τὰς ἐνεργείας καὶ τὰς ἡσυχίας, μέγεθος οὕτω καὶ ἀραιότητα περὶ τὴν ὅλην ἀναπνοὴν ἐργάζονται. ἆρ’ οὖν ἡμεῖς μὲν οὕτω φαμὲν, Ἱπποκράτης δ’ ἄλλως ἐγίγνωσκεν; ἢ κᾀκεῖνος ἐν προγνωστικῷ τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἔγραψεν; μέγα δὲ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ πολλοῦ χρόνου, παραφροσύνην σημαίνει. ὃ γὰρ ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν ἀραιὸν ὠνόμασε, τοῦτ’ ἐν τῷ προγνωστικῷ διὰ πολλοῦ κέκληκε, εἰς λόγον μεταβαλὼν τοὔνομα. καὶ γὰρ οὖν κᾀπὶ τῶν ἀῤῥώστων ἐκείνων, οἷς μέγα καὶ ἀραιόν φησι γίγνεσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα, πρὸς τοῖς ἄλλοις συμπτώμασι καὶ παραφροσύνην γράφει, ἐπὶ μὲν τοῦ πρώτου πάντων τοῦ Φιλίσκου τόνδε τὸν τρόπον. νύκτα δύσφορος, οὐκ ἐκοιμήθη, πάντα παρέκρουσε. οὕτως μὲν ἐν τῇ τῆς τρίτης ἡμέρας διηγήσει. ἐν δὲ τῇ τῆς πέμπτης πάλιν· ὕπνοι σμικροὶ, λόγοι, λῆρος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Just as, then, those who are delirious, having often asked for a chamber-pot in order to urinate, forgot in the meantime, and then would not urinate unless 831 someone reminded them, so, since breathing too is a psychic activity, and their reasoning has been impaired, they become more forgetful both regarding the activities themselves and regarding their intermissions. Doing both, then, over a long time, both the activities and the pauses, they thus produce largeness and rarity in the whole respiration. Is it, then, that we say so, but Hippocrates judged otherwise? Or did he too write in the Prognostic in this manner: 'breath drawn large and at long intervals signifies delirium'? For what he called 'rare' in the first book of the Epidemics, this he has called 'at long intervals' in the Prognostic, changing the word into a phrase. And indeed, in the case of those patients in whom he says the breath became large and rare, in addition to the other symptoms he also writes down delirium; in the case of the first of all, Philiscus, in this manner: 'During the night distressed, did not sleep, was delirious in every respect.' So in the account of the third day. And in that of the fifth again: 'short sleeps, talking, raving.'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λῆρος, πάντα παρέκρουσε, εἰς λόγον μεταβαλὼν τοὔνομα, ψυχικόν

Just as, then, the delirious, having often asked for a chamber pot in order to urinate, forget it in the meantime, and then would not urinate unless 831 someone reminds them, so, since breathing too is a psychic function, and their reasoning has been damaged, they become rather forgetful both about the activities themselves and about the intervals between them. Doing both, then, over a long time, both the activities and the rests, they thus bring about largeness and infrequency in the breathing as a whole. Do we, then, say this, while Hippocrates thought otherwise? Or did he too write in the Prognostic in this way: 'Breathing that is large and at long intervals of time signifies delirium'? For what in the first book of the Epidemics he called 'infrequent', this in the Prognostic he has called 'at long intervals', changing the name into a phrase. And indeed in the case of those patients in whom he says the breath becomes large and infrequent, in addition to the other symptoms he also records delirium; in the case of the very first of all, Philiscus, in this way: 'Night uncomfortable; he did not sleep; he was wholly out of his wits.' So it is in the account of the third day. And again in that of the fifth: 'Short sleeps; talking; raving.'

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ δὲ τοῦ δευτέρου 832 τῶν ἀῤῥώστων, ᾧ Σιληνὸς ἦν ὄνομα, ἐν μὲν τῇ τῆς δευτέρας ἡμέρας διηγήσει, σμικρὰ παρέκρουσέ, φησιν, ἐν δὲ τῇ τῆς τρίτης, λόγοι πολλοὶ, γέλως, ᾠδὴ, κατέχειν οὐκ ἠδύνατο. εἶτ’ ἐπιφέρων ἐρεῖ, τετάρτη διὰ τῶν αὐτῶν. Φιλίσκος μὲν δὴ καὶ Σιληνὸς ὅτι παρεφρόνησαν, οὕτως ἐμνημόνευσεν. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς Δρομάδεω γυναικὸς, καὶ γὰρ καὶ ταύτην ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα ἀναπνεῦσαί φησι, τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἔγραψε. περὶ μέσης ἡμέρης πολλὰ παρέκρουσε, καὶ πάλιν ταχὺ σμικρὰ κατενόει. οὕτως μὲν οὖν ἐν τῇ πέμπτῃ τῶν ἡμερῶν. εἶτα ἐπιφέρει· νυκτὸς ἐπεκοιμήθη, παρέκρουσε. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ τὰ διὰ τῆς ἕκτης ἡμέρας γενόμενα γράφων ὧδέ πώς φησι· ἄκρεα ψυχρὰ, παρέκρουσεν. οὕτω μὲν οὖν ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν, ἔνθα μεγάλου καὶ ἀραιοῦ πνεύματος, ἐνταῦθα καὶ παραφροσύνης μνημονεύει· κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ διὰ τῶν ἑξῆς βιβλίων ἡμᾶς ὑπομιμνήσκει, μηδαμοῦ μεγάλου καὶ ἀραιοῦ πνεύματος μνημονεύων, ὅπου μη μεγάλης παραφροσύνης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπεκοιμήθη, κατενόει

In the case of the second 832 of the patients, whose name was Silenus, in the account of the second day he says, 'he was slightly delirious,' and in that of the third, 'much talking, laughter, singing; he could not restrain himself.' Then, adding to this, he will say, 'fourth day: through the same things.' That Philiscus and Silenus, then, were delirious, he recorded in this way. And in the case of the wife of Dromeades—for he says that she too breathed rare and large—he wrote in this manner: 'about midday she was much delirious, and again quickly she came a little to her senses.' So, then, on the fifth of the days. Then he adds: 'at night she slept somewhat; she was delirious.' And in the same manner, writing also what happened in the course of the sixth day, he says something like this: 'extremities cold; she was delirious.' Thus, then, in the first book of the Epidemics, where there is mention of large and rare breath, there he also mentions delirium; and in the same manner throughout the following books too he reminds us, nowhere mentioning large and rare breath where he does not mention great delirium.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διὰ τῶν αὐτῶν, σμικρὰ κατενόει, ἐπεκοιμήθη, Δρομάδεω

In the case of the second 832 of the patients, whose name was Silenus, in the account of the second day he says, 'he wandered slightly,' and in that of the third, 'much talk, laughter, singing; he could not be restrained.' Then, continuing, he will say, 'Fourth day: the same symptoms.' That Philiscus and Silenus were deranged, then, he recorded in this way. In the case of the wife of Dromeades (for of her too he says that she breathed sparse and large) he wrote as follows: 'Around midday she wandered much, and again quickly had slight understanding.' So he writes on the fifth of the days. Then he adds: 'At night she dozed a little; she wandered.' And in the same way, writing of what happened during the sixth day, he says roughly this: 'extremities cold; she wandered.' Thus in the first book of the Epidemics, wherever he mentions large and sparse breathing, there he also mentions derangement. In the same way, throughout the following books he reminds us of it, nowhere mentioning large and sparse breathing where he does not also mention great derangement.

833 Ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἐπὶ τῶν νοσούντων ἐθεάσατο πολλάκις Ἱπποκράτης τὴν τοιαύτην δύσπνοιαν ἐπὶ παραφροσύνῃ γινομένην, καὶ ὅτι καθόλου περὶ αὐτοῦ ἀπεφήνατο διὰ τοῦ προγνωστικοῦ, καὶ ταῦτα μὲν ἱκανῶς ἔχειν ἡγοῦμαι, βεβαιωθήσεται δ’ οὐδὲν ἧττον ἔτι καὶ διὰ τῶν ἑξῆς εἰρησομένων. καὶ γάρ τοι καὶ δίκαιον ἦν μὴ πάνυ πολλὰ λέγεσθαι περὶ αὐτῆς· ἀλλὰ διὰ τοὺς ἐν τοῖς περὶ φύσεως ἀναπνοῆς λογισμοῖς σφαλέντας, εἶθ’ ὑπὸ φιλονεικίας ἀναισχυντοῦντας, ἠναγκάσθημεν καὶ ἡμεῖς πλείω τοῦ προσήκοντος εἰπεῖν, εἴ πως αὐτοὺς δυνηθείημεν κᾂν νῦν γοῦν μετάξαι πρὸς τὸ βέλτιον. ἔστι δ’, ὡς ἔοικεν, οὐδεμία βαφὴ δευσοποιὸς οὕτως ὡς τὸ τῶν ἄνευ λογισμοῦ πρὸς τὰ μοχθηρὰ δόγματα πάθος. οὐ γὰρ ἔστι μεταπεῖσαι τοὺς ἐν αὐτοῖς τραφέντας, ὡς ἂν μήτ’ ἐν ταῖς ἀποδεικτικαῖς μεθόδοις γεγυμνασμένους μήθ’ ὅλως πεπορισμένους τι κριτήριον ἀληθῶν καὶ ψευδῶν, ἀλλὰ μηδ’ εἶναι βουλομένους τούς γε πλείστους αὐτῶν, καὶ ταύτην μισοῦντας τὴν σοφίαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

833 That, then, Hippocrates often observed in the sick this kind of dyspnoea arising in consequence of delirium, and that he made a universal pronouncement about it in the Prognostic — these points too I consider to stand sufficiently established, and they will be confirmed no less also through what is to be said next. For indeed it would have been right that not very much be said about it; but on account of those who have gone astray in their reasonings concerning the nature of respiration, and who then, out of contentiousness, behave shamelessly, we too have been compelled to say more than is fitting, in case we might somehow be able, at least now, to bring them over to the better. But there is, as it seems, no dye so fast as the attachment of those who, without reasoning, hold to bad doctrines. For it is not possible to persuade otherwise those who have been reared in them, inasmuch as they have neither been trained in the demonstrative methods nor have procured for themselves at all any criterion of things true and false, but — most of them, at any rate — do not even wish there to be one, and hate this very wisdom.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δευσοποιὸς, πάθος, μετάξαι, μηδ’ εἶναι βουλομένους

833 That Hippocrates, then, often observed in the sick this kind of difficulty of breathing occurring with delirium, and that he made a general declaration about it in the Prognostic, I consider sufficiently shown; but it will be confirmed no less by what is to be said next. For it is not possible to persuade otherwise those who have been reared in them, since they are neither trained in the demonstrative methods nor have provided themselves at all with any criterion of what is true and what is false. Greek (Kühn)

τῶν δὴ τοιούτων ποτέ τις ἄῤῥωστον ἡμῖν ἐπιδείξας παραφρονοῦντα μὲν, ἀλλὰ μήτε μέγα μήτ’ 834 ἀραιὸν ἀναπνέοντα, καταγελῶν, μᾶλλον δὲ καγχάζων ἀναισχύντως, καὶ διαπαίζων Ἱπποκράτην καὶ ἡμᾶς ἀπηλλάττετο, μηδὲ λόγον μεταδοὺς ἡμῖν, μηδ’ ἀνασχόμενος μηδὲν ἐπακοῦσαι, καίτοι διδάξειν ἐπαγγελλομένοις, ὡς οὐ μάχεται τὸ φαινόμενον οὔτε τοῖς πρὸς ἡμῶν λεγομένοις οὔτε τοῖς Ἱπποκράτει γεγραμμένοις. ὁ μὲν γὰρ τὸ μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν πνεῦμα παραφροσύνης εἶναί φησι γνώρισμα, οὐ μὴν εἴ τις παραφρονεῖ, πάντως τοῦτον ἀραιὸν ἀναπνεῖν καὶ μέγα, τά τε πρὸς ἡμῶν λεγόμενα ταὐτὸ τοῦτ’ ἐπαγγέλλεται, μηδὲν παραχαραττόντων ἡμῶν μηδὲ μεταποιούντων ὧν ἐκεῖνος ἔγραψεν, ἀλλὰ τὰς ἐξηγήσεις καὶ τὰς ἀποδείξεις μόνας προστιθέντων. εἰ δέ σοι νῦν, ἔφην, ὦ οὗτος, ἐξεύρηται τὸ πᾶσι τοῖς παραφρονοῦσιν ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα γίγνεσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα, σαφῶς ἐνδείκνυσαι μηδὲν τῶν Ἱπποκράτους ἀνεγνωκὼς, καίτοι προσποιεῖς γε καταγιγνώσκειν αὐτῶν, ὡς ἀνεγνωκὼς δή που πρότερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

One of such men, then, once showed us a patient who was delirious, yet breathing neither large nor 834 rare, and, laughing — or rather guffawing shamelessly — and making sport of Hippocrates and of us, he went off, without granting us any discussion or enduring to listen to anything, although we promised to teach him that the phenomenon does not conflict either with what is said by us or with what has been written by Hippocrates. For he says that large and rare breath is a mark of delirium, not, however, that if someone is delirious, this person in every case breathes rare and large; and what is said by us professes this very same thing, since we neither counterfeit nor alter anything of what he wrote, but add only the exegeses and the demonstrations. But if, my good man, I said, you have now discovered that in all who are delirious the breath becomes rare and large, you clearly show that you have read none of the writings of Hippocrates, although you indeed pretend to condemn them, as having, I suppose, read them previously.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσποιεῖς

Of such men, one once showed us a patient who was delirious, yet breathed neither large nor 834 sparse; laughing at us, or rather guffawing shamelessly, and mocking Hippocrates and us, he went off, neither allowing us a word nor bearing to listen to anything at all, although we offered to teach him that the phenomenon conflicts neither with what is said by us nor with what Hippocrates wrote. For Hippocrates says that large and sparse breathing is a sign of delirium, not, however, that if someone is delirious, he necessarily breathes sparse and large. What is said by us declares this very same thing, since we neither falsify nor alter anything of what he wrote, but add only the exegeses and the demonstrations. 'But if you have now discovered, you there,' I said, 'that in all the delirious the breathing becomes sparse and large, you plainly show that you have read nothing of Hippocrates' works, although you make a show of condemning them, as though, presumably, you had read them beforehand.'

Here, then, he has written one directly after the other that his breath was thin and diminished and that he was deranged, on one and the same day, this eighth.

ἆρ’ ὦ πρὸς Διὸς οὐκ ἤδη σαφές ἐστιν, ὡς οὐκ ἐπὶ πάσης 836 παραφροσύνης Ἱπποκράτης ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα φησὶ γίγνεσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα; εἰ μή τι νομίζεις ὦ οὗτος οὐδὲν διαφέρειν τοῦ μεγάλου τὸ μινυθῶδες. εἰ γὰρ τοῦτο, δῆλον, ὡς οὐδὲ τὸ μινύθειν οἷόν τί ἐστιν, οἶσθα, καὶ νομίζεις ταὐτὸν εἶναι τῷ αὐξάνειν αὐτό. μὴ τοίνυν παρ’ ἡμῶν τά γε τοιαῦτα μάνθανε, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ τοὺς τὰ πρῶτα γράμματα διδάσκοντας ἴθι. ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ ταῦτα οὐκ ἔμαθεν ὁ τὸ μινύθειν ἀγνοῶν, οὕτως οὐδὲ τὰ Ἱπποκράτους ἀνέγνω συγγράμματα. πάντως γὰρ ἂν ἐν αὐτοῖς μυριάκις εὗρε καὶ τὸ μινύθειν καὶ τὸ μινυθῆναι καὶ τὴν μινύθησιν κατὰ πάντας σχεδὸν ἀριθμοὺς καὶ γένη καὶ πτώσεις ἐγκεκλιμένα πανταχοῦ, τοὐναντίον αὐξήσει τε καὶ τῷ αὐξάνειν δηλοῦντα, ὥστε καὶ τὸ μινυθῶδες εἴη ἂν τὸ ἐναντίον τῷ ηὐξημένῳ. εἴπερ οὖν τὸ ηὐξημένον μέγα, τὸ μινυθῶδες μικρόν ἐστιν. οὕτως οὖν καὶ ὁπότε τὴν δευτέραν ἡμέραν διηγεῖτο τοῦ Πυθίωνος, βραχύπνοον αὐτὸν ὠνόμασε. ἡ γάρ τοι βραχύπνοια καὶ τὴν ἐνέργειαν αὐτὴν βραχεῖαν γίγνεσθαι δηλοῖ καὶ τὴν ἡσυχίαν· ὥσπερ ἡ μακρόπνοια κατὰ τοὐναντίον ἄμφω μεμηκύνθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Is it not then, by Zeus, already clear that Hippocrates does not say that in every 836 delirium the breath becomes rare and large? Unless, you there, you think that the diminished differs in no way from the large. For if that is so, it is clear that you do not even know what 'to diminish' is, and you think it the same as 'to increase'. Do not, then, learn such things from us, but go to those who teach the first letters. But just as the man who is ignorant of 'to diminish' did not learn these things, so neither has he read the writings of Hippocrates. For he would certainly have found in them countless times both 'to diminish' and 'to be diminished' and 'diminution', inflected everywhere in almost every number, gender and case, signifying the opposite of increase and of increasing, so that the diminished too would be the opposite of the increased. If, then, the increased is large, the diminished is small. So too, when he was narrating the second day of Pythion, he called him short-breathed. For short-breathing indicates that both the activity itself becomes short and also the rest; just as long-breathing, conversely, indicates that both are lengthened.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

Is it not then, by Zeus, already clear that Hippocrates does not say that the breath becomes sparse and large in every 836 derangement? Unless, you there, you think that the diminished (μινυθῶδες) does not differ at all from the large. For if that is so, it is plain that you do not even know what 'to diminish' (μινύθειν) is, and you think it the same as 'to increase'. Do not, then, learn such things from us at any rate, but go to those who teach the first letters. But just as the man who does not know 'to diminish' did not learn these things, so neither has he read the treatises of Hippocrates. For he would certainly have found in them countless times 'to diminish' (μινύθειν), 'to have been diminished' (μινυθῆναι) and 'diminution' (μινύθησις), inflected everywhere in almost all numbers, genders and cases, signifying the opposite of increase and of increasing, so that 'diminished' too would be the opposite of 'increased'. If, then, the increased is large, the diminished is small. So too, accordingly, when he was narrating the second day of Pythion, he called him short-breathed. For short-breathing indicates that both the activity itself and the rest become short, just as long-breathing, conversely, indicates that both have been lengthened.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And yet, even if only one of the two were indicated, the point that the breathing does not become large and rare in all those who are delirious would even so have been conceded. For rare and large breathing is long in both respects, both in the activity and in the rest; whereas short-breathing is in every case short in at least one of the two, just as thin and diminished breath is in every case short. Hippocrates himself, then, clearly knows that large and rare breathing does not of necessity belong to all those who are delirious; yet this does not conflict with his pronouncement concerning this kind of dyspnoea. For rare and large breathing is proper to delirium, and for this reason it will of necessity indicate it; it is not the case, however, that if someone is delirious he of necessity breathes large and rare.

Hippocrates himself, then, clearly knows that the large and sparse breath is not of necessity present in all who are delirious; yet this does not conflict with his pronouncement concerning this kind of difficulty of breathing. Greek (Kühn)

οὐ γὰρ ἀχώριστον, ἀλλὰ ἴδιον ἔφαμεν εἶναι παραφροσύνης τὸ ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα πνεῦμα, καθάπερ τὸ γεωμετρεῖν, τὸ τεκταίνεσθαι, τὸ φιλοσοφεῖν, τὸ ῥητορεύειν καὶ ἄλλα μυρία, ὧν ἕκαστον οὐδενὶ μὲν ὑπάρχει τῶν ἄλλων ζώων, ὅτι μὴ ἀνθρώπῳ μόνῳ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἴδιον αὐτοῦ λέγεται, οὐ 838 μὴν ἀχώριστόν γέ τι, καθάπερ τὸ λογικόν. οὐ γὰρ πᾶν ἴδιον εὐθὺς καὶ ἀχώριστον. ὥσπερ δὲ ταῦθ’ ἡμᾶς ἡ λοιδορουμένη πρὸς αὐτῶν ἐδίδαξεν διαλεκτικὴ, οὕτως καὶ ὅτι τὸ τοιοῦτον ἀξίωμα, εἰ μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἀναπνεῖ, παραφρονεῖ, οὔτε ταὐτόν ἐστι τῷ, εἰ παραφρονεῖ, μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἀναπνεῖ, οὔτ’ ἀναγκαῖον αὐτὰ συναληθεύεσθαι. οὐ γὰρ ἀντιστρέφει πρὸς ἄλληλα ταῦτα, ἀλλὰ μόνον ἀναστρέφει. ὡς οὖν οὐδεὶς Ὀλύμπια νικῆσαι δύναται μὴ πολλῷ πρότερον γυμνασάμενος, οὕτως οὐδὲ τῆς ἐν τοῖς λόγοις ἀληθείας ἐπιβοηθὸς γενέσθαι μὴ μαθὼν τὰ κᾀν τούτοις παλαίσματα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀναστρέφει, ἀντιστρέφει

For we said that rare and large breathing is not an inseparable attribute of delirium, but a property of it, just as doing geometry, practising carpentry, philosophising, practising rhetoric, and countless other things, each of which belongs to none of the other animals except man alone, and for this reason is called a property of him, yet 838 not something inseparable, as the rational is. For not every property is straightaway also inseparable. And just as dialectic, which is reviled by them, taught us these things, so too it taught us that an axiom of this sort, 'if he breathes large and rare, he is delirious', is neither the same as 'if he is delirious, he breathes large and rare', nor is it necessary that they be true together. For these do not convert (ἀντιστρέφειν) with one another, but only invert (ἀναστρέφειν). So then, just as no one can win at the Olympic games without having trained long beforehand, so neither can anyone become a defender of the truth in arguments without having learned the wrestling-holds that exist in these too.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναστρέφει, ἀντιστρέφει, παραφροσύνης

For we said that sparse and large breathing is not inseparable from derangement but proper to it, just as are geometrising, carpentering, philosophising, practising oratory and countless other things. Each of these belongs to none of the other animals, but to man alone, and for this reason is called a property of him, yet it is 838 not something inseparable from him, as being rational is. For not every property is thereby also inseparable. And just as dialectic, which is reviled by them, taught us these things, so it also taught us that such a proposition, 'if he breathes large and sparse, he is deranged', is neither the same as 'if he is deranged, he breathes large and sparse', nor is it necessary that they be true together. For these do not convert with one another, but are only inverted. So, as no one can win at Olympia without having trained for a long time beforehand, so no one can become a defender of truth in arguments without having learned the wrestling holds in these as well.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πάλιν

And just as those who are wholly ignorant of wrestling-holds do not even know how to fall gracefully, but sometimes, lying on their backs on the ground, keep their grip and throttle and do not let go, in the same way, I think, those who are ignorant of the art that concerns arguments, having often manifestly fallen a most shameful fall, are so far from feeling shame that they even think they have won; as indeed does the man who points to a deranged patient breathing small, and then thinks that by means of this 839 phenomenon the pronouncement of Hippocrates has been refuted. This man, at any rate, having fallen a most shameful fall, thought he had thrown his opponent. For how is it not most shameful, on the one hand to be found to have read nothing of the writings of Hippocrates, and for this reason to think that what was written by him elsewhere again was said emptily; and on the other hand not even to understand what is said, but to mishear and to think that the statement that large and rare breathing is a sign of derangement is the same as the statement that the aforesaid dyspnoea follows derangement in every case? Then what wonder is it that those who do not know even matters so small as these are blind in their reasonings about cures? But these small matters evidently depend on other matters, themselves also small, yet neglected by them. For not knowing which faculty governs the respiratory activity is the origin of such errors; and that was not known because it was not known beforehand whence one must procure the premises for demonstrations.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For it is in this wrestling-bout that those who have been left untrained, and those who are utterly ignorant, suffer falls of such magnitude; and it has been demonstrated by us elsewhere, and especially in the second book On the Movement of the Muscles, 840 that those who said that respiration is an involuntary activity happened to go astray because they were unable to derive the premises for the demonstration from the very essence of the matter, but followed the other things as accidents. But Hippocrates, who looked at the very nature of things and disregarded the others as accidents, in general pronounced firmly, through the Prognostic: 'breathing that is large and at long intervals signifies delirium'; and in particular, through what appears in diseases, he tested the statement with the most extreme tests for exactness, which, I think, one would marvel at, if one were willing to apply one's mind to what is said; for one will learn that upon all deliria there follows large and rare breath according to their own proper account, not however in all who are delirious, but only in those who do not, because of some other condition greater than the delirium, have their respiration altered in a different way.

But let him first allow me to add the account of one sick woman, recorded in the third book of the Epidemics, both for the demonstration of what has already been said and for the discovery 841 of what now lies before us. He begins his account of her in the following manner: 'In Thasos the wife of Dealces, who lay sick on the Leion, was seized by an acute fever with shivering, arising from grief; from the beginning she kept wrapping herself up, and throughout to the end, always silent, she groped about, plucked, scratched, picked at hairs; tears and again laughter; she did not sleep; irritation from the bowels, nothing passed; she drank small amounts when reminded; urines scanty, thin, having no sediment; fevers slight to the hand; coldness of the extremities. On the ninth day she raved much, and again settled down, silent.' So then, that from the beginning and throughout to the end this woman was delirious, we have learned; for not one single form of delirium befell her, nor a trivial one, but many and strong ones, some while she was silent, others while she was speaking, and some with tears, others indeed with laughter. Let us next hear Hippocrates too, as to what form of dyspnoea he says occurred in her. He writes thus: 'On the fourteenth day the breath was rare, large, at long intervals, and again she was short-breathed.'

But let me first be permitted to add the account of one sick woman, written in the third book of the Epidemics, both as a demonstration of what has already been said and at the same time for the discovery 841 of what is now before us. Let us next hear Hippocrates too, as to what sort of form of difficulty of breathing he says arose in her. He writes as follows: 'On the fourteenth day breathing rare, large, at long intervals; and again she was short-breathed.'

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Here, at any rate, "short-breathed" is clearly said once again by way of opposition to the woman who has great and rare 842 breath, and no one any longer, not even if he were altogether the most obtuse, would be in doubt that Hippocrates does not say that in all who are delirious the respiration becomes rare and great. At any rate, in the case of one and the same woman he mentions each of the two kinds of difficult breathing in succession, and proceeding a little further he writes somewhat as follows: "On the twentieth, much talking, and again she settled down, voiceless, short-breathed." Then, having said, "on the twenty-first she died," at the end of the whole account he has added the following: "In this woman throughout the breath was rare, great; she was insensible of everything; she kept wrapping herself up." It is clear, then, from everything we have cited, that Hippocrates knew that sometimes the great and rare breath occurs in those who are delirious, sometimes its opposite, and sometimes both, befalling the same person in turn. And I think I shall show you, if you apply your mind and do not listen carelessly to what is said, that in the very patients whom he described, the cause of each of the aforesaid differences is indicated by him.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνδεικνυμένην αὐτῷ, ἱδρύνθη, ἀναισθήτως πάντων εἶχεν, περιεστέλλετο

Here, at any rate, the short-breathed woman is again clearly spoken of 842 by antithesis to the woman who has large and sparse breath. No one any longer, not even if he were altogether the most obtuse of men, could be in doubt that Hippocrates does not say that the breathing becomes sparse and large in all who are deranged. At any rate, in the case of one and the same woman he mentions both kinds of difficulty of breathing in succession. Going on a little further, he writes something like this: 'On the twentieth, much talk, and again she settled down, voiceless, short-breathed.' Then, having said 'on the twenty-first she died', at the end of the whole account he has added the following: 'In this woman, throughout, breath sparse, large; she was insensible of everything; she was always wrapping herself up.' From all the passages we have cited, then, it is clear that Hippocrates knew that in the deranged the large and sparse breath sometimes occurs, sometimes its opposite, and sometimes both befall the same person in turn. And I think I shall show you, if you pay attention and do not listen to what is said cursorily, that in the very patients about whom he wrote the cause of each of the aforementioned differences is indicated by him.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνεκαλεῖτο, λεῖμμα

I shall begin from the case of illness last 843 cited by us, that of the wife of Dealces, concerning which, having completed the whole account, and then, as is his custom, recapitulating briefly the chief points that hold it together, he writes as follows: "In this woman throughout the breath was rare, large; she was without perception of all things." For one among all these things is also the need for respiration, and this too she neglected for a longer time, because she perceived nothing; and as in this a long interval of rest was produced, the breath became rare, the choice setting out toward the activity only then for the first time, when it came near to destruction and suffocation. And just as this woman did not drink sufficiently—for she drank small amounts, he says, when people reminded her—so neither did she breathe before she was reminded by the force of being suffocated, which pricks and goads and rouses to the activity far more violently than those who remind one to drink. But having begun to breathe, she nonetheless recalled the whole previous deficit by the largeness of the activity, like those who sigh in griefs and in weeping.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀφικνῆται, ἡσυχίας, συνέχοντα, λεῖμμα

I shall begin from the illness of the wife of Dealces, the last 843 cited by us. In her case, having completed the whole account, he then, as is his custom, briefly recapitulates the chief points that hold it together and writes as follows: 'In this woman, throughout, the breath was sparse, large; she was insensible of everything.' For the need for respiration too is one of 'everything', and this too she neglected for a considerable time, because she perceived nothing. And since in the meantime a long pause was produced, the breath became sparse, the choice setting out towards the activity for the first time only when it comes near to destruction and suffocation. And just as this woman did not drink sufficiently ('for she drank little,' he says, 'when people reminded her'), so neither did she breathe until she was reminded by the violence that goes with being suffocated, which pricks and goads her and rouses her to the activity far more vehemently than those reminding her to drink. And once she had begun to breathe, she nonetheless recovered the whole preceding deficit by the magnitude of the activity, like those who sigh in griefs and in weeping.

ἀλλ’ ἐκεῖνοι μὲν ὑπὸ διττῆς αἰτίας τοῦτο πάσχουσιν, ὅτι τε περὶ τὸ φροντιζόμενον, ἐφ’ ᾧ λυποῦνται, καταγίγνονται, 844 καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀραιότερόν τε καὶ μικρότερον ἀναπνεύσαντες ἐπὶ πολλαῖς ἐνεργείας ἐφεξῆς, ἔπειτα βιασθέντες ὑπὸ τοῦ λείποντος, ἅπαν εἰσάπαξ ἠναγκάσθησαν αὐτὸ πληρῶσαι· καὶ προσέτι, διότι κατὰ τὰς λύπας ἔσω κινεῖται τό τε πνεῦμα καὶ τὸ θερμὸν, ἐπὶ δὲ ταῖς τοιαύταις διαθέσεσι λεληθότως τε καὶ κατὰ βραχὺ πολλὴ κατὰ τοῦ βάθους ἀθροιζομένη θερμότης ὁπότε πρῶτον ἀνιάσῃ τὸν ἄνθρωπον, ἀνάγκη μεῖζον ἀναπνεῦσαι. ἐν δὲ ταῖς κατὰ τοὺς παραφρονοῦντας διαθέσεσιν ἡ μὲν ἀπὸ τῆς πρώτης αἰτίας ἀνάγκη παραπλήσιος, ἡ δ’ ἀπὸ τῆς δευτέρας οὐκέθ’ ὑπάρχει. οὐ γὰρ δὴ ἔσω γε μᾶλλον ἢ ἔξω κινεῖται τούτοις τὸ θερμὸν, εἰ μή τι ἄλλο κατὰ τύχην προσγένηται σύμπτωμα. διαφέρουσι δὲ καὶ τῷ πυρέττειν οἱ παραπαίοντες τῶν ἄλλως λυπουμένων. καὶ τοίνυν καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης εὑρίσκεται χρώμενος τῷ τῆς ἀνακλήσεως ὀνόματι, καθάπερ ἄλλοτε καὶ περὶ Φιλίσκου λέγων· τούτῳ τὸ πνεῦμα διὰ τέλεος ὥσπερ ἀνακαλεομένῳ. τί ποτ’ οὖν ἐστι τὸ διὰ χρόνου, καὶ δὴ τοῦτ’ ἐξηγήσασθαι δίκαιον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐνεργείας (ἐπὶ πολλαῖς ἐνεργείας, probably for ἐνεργείαις), ἀνακαλεομένῳ

But those people suffer this from a twofold cause: both because they are absorbed in the object of their concern, over which they grieve, 844 and on this account, having breathed more sparsely and more shallowly over many successive activities (ἐπὶ πολλαῖς ἐνεργείας), and then being forced by what is lacking, they have been compelled to make up the whole of it all at once; and, in addition, because in states of grief both the pneuma and the heat move inward, and in such conditions a great deal of heat, gathered imperceptibly and little by little in the depth, when it first distresses the person, necessarily makes him breathe more largely. But in the conditions of those who are delirious, the necessity arising from the first cause is similar, whereas that arising from the second no longer obtains. For in these people the heat certainly does not move inward more than outward, unless some other symptom happens by chance to supervene. Those who are deranged also differ from those who are grieving in the ordinary way by the fact that they have fever. And indeed Hippocrates himself is found using the term 'recalling' (ἀνάκλησις), as on another occasion also, when speaking about Philiscus: 'in this man the breathing throughout was as if he were recalling it.' What, then, 'at intervals' (διὰ χρόνου) means—this too it is right to explain.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπὶ πολλαῖς ἐνεργείας ἐφεξῆς (ἐνεργείας perhaps for ἐνεργείαις), καταγίγνονται, ἀνακαλεομένῳ, διὰ χρόνου

But those persons suffer this from a twofold cause: first, because they are wholly absorbed in the thing they are anxious about, over which they grieve, 844 and for this reason, having breathed more rarely and smaller over many successive activities, they are then forced by the deficit and compelled to make it all up at once; and further, because in griefs both the pneuma and the heat move inward, and in such conditions, when the abundant heat that collects in the depth imperceptibly and little by little first distresses the person, he must breathe larger. In the conditions of those who are delirious, the necessity arising from the first cause is similar, but that arising from the second no longer exists. For in them the heat does not move inward rather than outward, unless some other symptom happens by chance to be added. Those who are deranged also differ from those who grieve for other reasons in that they are feverish. And indeed Hippocrates himself is found using the term 'recall' (anaklēsis), as elsewhere too when he says of Philiscus: 'in him the breath was throughout as of one recalling it.' What, then, is meant by 'at intervals' (dia chronou)? It is right to explain this as well.

εἰ γὰρ ἐλλείποι τὸ πολλοῦ, ταὐτὸν δύναται τὸ διὰ χρόνου τῷ κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν εἰρημένῳ, μέγα δ’ ἀναπνεόμενον καὶ διὰ ﻿ 845 πολλοῦ χρόνου, παραφροσύνην σημαίνει. ἀλλ’ εἰ τοῦτο, δὶς ἂν ἐφεξῆς εἴη ταὐτὸ λεγόμενον ἐπί τε τῆς Δεάλκους γυναικὸς καὶ τοῦ ἐν Μελιβοίῃ νεανίσκου. γράφει οὖν ἐπ’ ἀμφοῖν ὡσαύτως· πνεῦμα ἀραιὸν, μέγα, διὰ χρόνου· εἰ τὸ πολλοῦ προστεθείη, γένοιτ’ ἂν τὸ λεγόμενον τοιοῦτον· πνεῦμα ἀραιὸν, μέγα, διὰ χρόνου πολλοῦ· καὶ δὶς ἂν εἴη λεγόμενον τὸ ἀραιόν. οὐκ οὖν ἐλλείπειν ἡγητέον τὸ πολλοῦ, ἢ οὐχ οὕτως ἐλλείπειν, ὡς καὶ νῦν εἴπομεν, ἀλλ’ ἑτέρως τε καὶ καθ’ ἕτερον τρόπον, ἵνα ᾖ τὸ λεγόμενον τοιοῦτον· πνεῦμα μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν, οὐ συνεχῶς οὐδ’ ἐφεξῆς, ἀλλὰ διὰ χρόνου πλείονος; εἰ μὲν γὰρ συνεχῶς καὶ κατὰ τὸ ἑξῆς ἁπάσας τὰς ἀναπνοὰς συμπέσοι γενέσθαι μεγάλας τε καὶ ἀραιὰς, μὴ προστιθέναι ταῖς τοιαύταις αὐτὸν τὸ διὰ χρόνου, ἀλλ’ ἁπλῶς γράφειν, πνεῦμα μέγα καὶ ἀραιόν· εἰ δ’ αἱ μὲν πλείους τῶν ἀναπνοῶν μὴ γίγνοιντο μεγάλαι καὶ ἀραιαὶ, παρεμπίπτοιεν δέ τινες ὀλίγαι τοιαῦται, τότ’ οὐκέθ’ ἁπλῶς αὐτὸν ἀραιὸν καὶ μέγα λέγειν, ἀλλὰ τὸ διὰ χρόνου προστιθέναι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διὰ χρόνου

For if 'much' (πολλοῦ) were omitted, the phrase 'at intervals' (διὰ χρόνου) could mean the same as what is said in the Prognostic: 'breath that is breathed large and at 845 long intervals signifies derangement.' But if this were so, the same thing would be said twice in succession in the case of the wife of Dealces and of the young man in Meliboea. For he writes in the same way concerning both: 'breath rare, large, at intervals.' If 'much' were added, what is said would become as follows: 'breath rare, large, at long intervals'; and 'rare' would then be said twice. Are we not, then, to suppose that 'much' is omitted—or rather, not omitted in the way we have just said, but otherwise and in another manner, so that what is said amounts to this: 'breath large and rare, not continuously nor in succession, but at longer intervals'? For if it happened that all the respirations continuously and in sequence became large and rare, we are to suppose that he does not add 'at intervals' to such cases, but writes simply 'breath large and rare'; whereas if the majority of the respirations did not become large and rare, but some few of that kind occurred intermittently, then he no longer calls it simply rare and large, but adds 'at intervals.'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διὰ χρόνου, οὐκ οὖν (possibly οὐκοῦν)

For if 'long' were missing, 'at intervals' would mean the same as what is said in the Prognostic: 'breathing that is large and at 845 long intervals of time signifies delirium.' But if this is so, the same thing would be said twice in succession in the cases of both the wife of Dealces and the young man at Meliboea. For he writes in the same way for both: 'breath sparse, large, at intervals'. If 'long' were added, what is said would become: 'breath sparse, large, at long intervals', and 'sparse' would be said twice. Must we then hold that 'long' is not missing, or that it is missing not in the way we have just stated but otherwise and after a different manner, so that what is said is this: 'breath large and sparse, not continuously nor in succession, but at a longer interval'? For if all the breaths happened to be large and sparse continuously and in succession, he would not add 'at intervals' to such breaths but would write simply 'breath large and sparse'. But if most of the breaths were not large and sparse, and only a few such breaths fell in among them, then he would no longer say simply 'sparse and large' but would add 'at intervals'.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Or does what is said about the wife of Dealces 846 at the end of the whole account conflict with this too? For he says this: "breathing throughout rare and great"; and yet higher up, at the fourteenth day, he said: "breathing rare and great, at intervals." For clearly what he here called "at intervals" (διὰ χρόνου), this in what follows he has called "throughout" (διὰ τέλεος). What, then, is "throughout"? Evidently that which occurs up to the end, through the whole of the disease. But with this what is said at the fourteenth day would seem to conflict: "breathing rare and great at intervals, and again short-breathed." For how, if through the whole time the breathing was great and rare, has "and again short-breathed" been added? For if there was any whole period when she was short-breathed, she was not long-breathed throughout. And indeed, that he meant her to have been long-breathed throughout, even if not through this expression at the fourteenth day, yet at least at the end, when he said "throughout," is clearly shown, and it will be agreed by all who are accustomed to listening to Hippocrates' diction.

For clearly what he here called 'at intervals', this in what follows he has called 'throughout'. What, then, is 'throughout' (διὰ τέλεος)? Plainly, that which occurs up to the end, through the whole 846 of the disease. But what was said at the fourteenth day would seem to conflict with this: 'breath sparse and large at intervals, and again short-breathed.' For if there was some whole period of time when she was short-breathed, she was not long-breathed throughout. And indeed that he meant her to have been long-breathed throughout is clearly shown, if not by this phrase at the fourteenth day, at least at the end, when he said 'throughout'; and this will be agreed by all who are accustomed to hearing the diction of Hippocrates.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μήτε τοίνυν οὐδ’ ἄπορόν ἐστιν

For not once or twice but very many times is the expression 'through to the end' (dia teleos) found in him, and it is applied in 847 no other case than when he wishes to indicate 'throughout the whole time'. So this remains in every way a difficulty, whether we suppose that 'through to the end' itself indicates 'at intervals', or something else. And yet perhaps it is not even a difficulty, but we are vexing ourselves in vain. For what intervenes rarely does not, in any of the things so stated, interrupt the pronouncement made in universal terms—just as if we were also to speak thus: in this woman the breath was large and rare throughout the whole time, but occasionally, as a rare occurrence, short-breathing intervened. And that such a usage is customary with Hippocrates himself can be shown both from the other books and from the third book of the Epidemics itself, in which this patient too is recorded. For when narrating the pestilential constitution he began somewhat as follows: 'a year southerly, rainy, windlessness through to the end.' And proceeding a little further he says: 'the Etesian winds blew slight and intermittent.'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μήτε (μήτε τοίνυν οὐδ’ ἄπορόν ἐστιν: probably a corruption of μήποτε, 'perhaps', and translated so), διακόπτει (taken as 'breaks up', i.e. invalidates), ἐν οὐδενὶ τῶν οὕτως λεγομένων (construal uncertain)

For not once or twice, but very often, the expression 'throughout' (διὰ τέλεος) is found in him, and it is not applied 847 to any one other thing than when he wishes to indicate 'through the whole time'. So this point remains wholly perplexing, whether we suppose that 'throughout' (διὰ τέλεος) itself signifies 'for a time' (διὰ χρόνου) or something else. Perhaps, then, it is not even perplexing, and we are harassing ourselves in vain. For what rarely intrudes does not, in any statement made in this manner, break up the assertion that is made in general terms; it is just as if we were to say: in this woman the breathing was large and rare through the whole time, but occasionally, as a rare event, short breathing intruded. That such a usage is customary for Hippocrates himself can be shown both from his other books and from the third book of the Epidemics itself, in which this patient too has been recorded. For in setting out the pestilential constitution he began roughly thus: 'A southerly year, rainy, windlessness throughout (διὰ τέλεος).' And having gone on a little, he says: 'The etesian winds blew slight and intermittent.'

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διὰ παρηγορίης, (ὅτι)

But he called the year windless for this very reason, that neither any other winds nor the so-called Etesian winds blew continuously and to any noteworthy degree, but slightly and in a scattered fashion. 848 And when writing of Apollonius at Abdera, the thirteenth case after this constitution, once he had discussed all his days during the disease, he added at the end of the whole account: "delirious dia teleos"; and yet a little above he had written about him in these very words: "About the twenty-fourth day, by way of alleviation; in other respects in the same condition, but he was in his senses for a little while." But his being in his senses for a little while was not sufficient to overturn the statement concerning the patient as a whole, and for this reason he rightly said "delirious dia teleos," that is, up to the end, which has the same force as "throughout the whole disease." Accordingly—for it is now necessary to put a head upon the argument—(it is established that) dia chronou is not the same as dia teleos.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διὰ παρηγορίης, παντὸς τοῦ νοσοῦντος, τὸ (ὅτι), κατενόησεν

But he called the year windless for this very reason: that neither any other winds nor those called the etesians blew continuously and to any considerable degree, but only slightly and intermittently. 848 And when writing of Apollonius in Abdera, the thirteenth case after this constitution, once he had gone through all the days of his illness, he added at the end of the whole account: 'delirious throughout'. Yet a little above he had written about him in these very words: 'About the twenty-fourth, by way of soothing treatment (διὰ παρηγορίης): the rest in the same state, but he understood a little.' But his understanding a little was not enough to overturn the pronouncement made about the patient as a whole, and for this reason he rightly said 'delirious throughout', that is, 'until the end', which amounts to the same as 'through the whole of the disease'. Therefore (for it is now necessary to put a head on the argument) 'at intervals' (διὰ χρόνου) is not the same as 'throughout' (διὰ τέλεος).

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And since this has been shown, let us next state the cause on account of which in some of those who are delirious the breath becomes rare and large throughout the whole disease, while in some it never does; and in some [it is so] for the greater part of the time, with either the breath that is simply different, or even the contrary one, intervening only rarely, whereas in others, conversely, the large and rare [breath occurs] rarely, 849 and frequently either the contrary one or the one that differs in whatever way; and in some, each equally. Let us here again recall what was demonstrated in the preceding book concerning the simple conditions. For pain alone, arising in one of the parts moved in the respirations, was shown to render the whole respiration small and frequent; delirium alone, the one contrary to it, the rare and large; increase of the heat, the large and frequent; and cooling, the contrary of this, the small and rare. If, then, the order of the respiration is confounded by delirium alone, the breath will be large and rare throughout; if by pain alone, small and frequent; but if both come together, then, when the pain prevails strongly, the alteration arising from the delirium is wholly imperceptible; but when the pain is moderate, it will come about that the respiration is altered either more from the pain and less from the delirium, or the reverse, or equally from both.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

And since this has been shown, let us next state the cause on account of which, in some of those who are deranged, the breath becomes sparse and large throughout the whole disease, and in some never; and in some it is so for the greater part of the time, with something infrequently intervening that is either simply different or even contrary, while in others, conversely, the large and sparse breath occurs infrequently 849, but frequently either the contrary or what is different in any way whatsoever; and in others each equally. Here too let us again recall what was demonstrated in the previous book concerning the simple conditions. For pain alone, arising in one of the parts that are moved in breathing, was shown to make the whole breathing small and frequent; derangement alone to make the opposite of this, sparse and large; increase of the heat to make it large and frequent; and cooling, the opposite of that, small and sparse. If, then, the order of the breathing were confounded by derangement alone, the breath will be large and sparse throughout; if by pain alone, small and frequent; and if both come together, then when the pain prevails strongly, the alteration arising from the derangement is entirely imperceptible; but when it is moderate, it will happen that the breathing is altered either more by the pain and less by the derangement, or the reverse, or equally by both.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προαιρέσεως, δοκοῦσι

For derangement is not at all able completely to efface the alteration arising from pain, except if it itself should be 850 great and exceedingly vehement, while the pain is small and faint; pain, however, when it is strong, completely overcomes the dyspnoea arising from derangement, so that it does not appear at all. So too, if derangement is combined with some one of the other conditions, it will produce now one form of respiration, now another, according as it prevails, or is prevailed over, or is of equal strength, or now, as it may happen, prevails, and again at another time of the disease is prevailed over. We shall show, then, that in the books of the Epidemics too he teaches this very thing through the patients recorded in them. And it will be shown evidently and clearly once we have first said a little about the purpose of Hippocrates. For the ancients suppose that he writes down everything that happens to the sick, as Thucydides also does; but it is not so; rather, this very thing is what is most opposite in the writings of Hippocrates as compared with those of Thucydides.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

For delirium is not altogether able to obliterate completely the alteration arising from the pain, unless it should itself be 850 great and exceedingly violent, and the pain small and faint; the pain, however, when it is strong, completely overcomes the difficulty of breathing arising from the delirium, so that it does not show itself at all. In the same way, if delirium is combined with any of the other conditions, it will produce now one form of breathing and now another, according as it prevails, or is prevailed over, or is of equal strength, or at one time, as it may happen, prevails, and again at another moment of the disease is prevailed over. ὁ μὲν γὰρ πάντα γράφει τὰ καὶ τοῖς ἰδιώταις γνώριμα, μηδὲν ὅλως αὐτῶν παραλιπὼν, ὁ Θουκυδίδης, ὁ δ’ Ἱπποκράτης ὀλίγα μὲν τούτων, ὅσα πρὸς τὴν ὅλην διάθεσιν, καθ’ ἣν ἐκινδύνευσεν ὁ κάμνων, διαφέρει, 851 πάμπολλα δ’ ἄλλα τοῖς ἰδιώταις μὲν παρεωραμένα, τεχνικὴν δὲ πάνυ καὶ ἀκριβῆ τὴν διάγνωσιν παρεχόμενα, καὶ δυνάμενα πολλάκις καὶ αὐτοὺς τοὺς ἀρίστους ἰατροὺς λαθεῖν. οὗτος δ’ ὢν ταχὺς ἐν τῷ ἑρμηνεύειν, καὶ μηδὲν ὅ τι μὴ καὶ μεγάλως ὀνήσει γράφειν ἀξιῶν, δόξει σοι πάλιν ἐξεστάναι τῶν οἰκείων φρενῶν, ὅταν τὰ μηδ’ ὅλως γενόμενα τῷ νοσοῦντι γράφῃ, τοῦτ’ αὐτὸ προσδηλῶν, ὅτι μὴ ἐγένετο. ὁ γὰρ καὶ τῶν γενομένων τὰ πλεῖστα παραλείπων οὐκ ἂν δόξειε τοῖς μὴ γενομένοις διατρίβων παραπαίειν; ἀλλ’ εἰ ἐνόεις ὅτι διὰ τὴν αὐτὴν αἰτίαν κᾀκεῖνα παραλείπει καὶ ταῦτα γράφει, τότ’ οἶμαί σε θαυμάσειν αὐτόν. οὐδὲ γὰρ τὸ τυχὸν, οὐδ’ εἰς τὸν τυχόντα δυνάμενον ἰατρὸν ἐλθεῖν ἐκεῖνος ἔγραψεν. ἀποδέδεικται δὲ καὶ περὶ τούτων ἡμῖν ἐν ἄλλοις τε πολλοῖς καὶ δὴ κᾀν τοῖς περὶ τῆς Ἱπποκράτους ἀνατομῆς, οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ κᾀν τοῖς περὶ τοῦ παρὰ τοῦ Θουκυδίδου λοιμοῦ. τούτων τοίνυν μεμνημένος, οὐ χαλεπῶς αἰσθήσῃ τῶν λεγομένων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ταχὺς ἐν τῷ ἑρμηνεύειν

For the one, Thucydides, writes everything that is known even to laymen, omitting none of it at all, whereas Hippocrates writes few of these things—only as many as make a difference to the whole condition in which the patient was in danger— 851 but a very great many other things, overlooked by laymen, yet furnishing a thoroughly technical and precise diagnosis, and often capable of escaping even the best physicians themselves. But this man, being brief in his manner of expression and thinking it right to write nothing that will not also be of great benefit, will seem to you, conversely, to have departed from his proper wits when he writes down things that did not happen at all to the sick man, indicating this very point, that it did not happen. For would not one who omits even most of the things that did happen seem to be raving when he dwells on things that did not happen? But if you understood that for the same cause he both omits the former and writes the latter, then I think you would admire him. For that man wrote nothing that is commonplace, nor anything that could occur to a commonplace physician. These matters too have been demonstrated by us both in many other works and in particular in those on the anatomy of Hippocrates, and not least also in those on the plague in Thucydides. Keeping these things in mind, then, you will perceive what is being said without difficulty.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταχὺς ἐν τῷ ἑρμηνεύειν, οὐδ’ εἰς τὸν τυχόντα δυνάμενον ἰατρὸν ἐλθεῖν

For the one, Thucydides, writes everything that is familiar even to laymen, leaving out none of it at all, whereas Hippocrates writes few of these things, only those that bear upon the whole condition in which the patient was in danger, 851 but a great many other things which are overlooked by laymen, yet which furnish a thoroughly technical and precise diagnosis, and which can often escape the notice even of the best physicians themselves. And he, being swift in expression, and thinking fit to write nothing that will not also be of great benefit, will on the other hand seem to you to have departed from his own wits when he writes things that did not happen to the patient at all, making clear this very point, that it did not happen. For would not one who leaves out most even of the things that happened seem to be deranged in spending time on things that did not happen? But if you understood that it is for the same reason that he both leaves out those things and writes these, then, I think, you would admire him. For he wrote nothing chance, nothing that could occur to just any physician. This too has been demonstrated by us both in many other works and in particular in the books On the Anatomy of Hippocrates, and not least in those On the Plague in Thucydides. Keeping these things in mind, then, you will without difficulty perceive what is being said.

For I am going to show, even if it will seem paradoxical to someone, that many of those written up in the Epidemics breathed with difficulty, yet that Hippocrates says nothing about their 852 dyspnoea, because it was clear and evident to all physicians. For if it happened that someone's sternum, or ribs, or the parts about the collarbones and the pharynx became inflamed, and on this account he of necessity breathed small and frequent, as was demonstrated in the preceding book, Hippocrates would not have thought it worth writing either the kind of the dyspnoea or, in the first place, that he became dyspnoeic. For such a kind of dyspnoea is very common, being seen almost every day in peripneumonic and pleuritic and synanchic patients, and in all, simply, in whom something in the thorax and the lung is inflamed. And upon inflammations of the liver too, and especially those at its convex parts, and those of the spleen, and those at its head, and those of the peritoneum, this kind of dyspnoea follows.

For I am going to show, even if it will seem paradoxical to someone, that many of those written up in the Epidemics suffered difficulty of breathing, and yet Hippocrates said nothing about their 852 difficulty of breathing, because it was clear and evident to all physicians. For if it happened that someone's sternum, or ribs, or the parts about the collarbones and the pharynx became inflamed, and that on this account he necessarily breathed small and frequent, as was shown in the preceding book, Hippocrates would not have thought it worth writing down either the form of the difficulty of breathing or at all the fact that the patient had become dyspnoeic. For this form of difficulty of breathing is common, being seen almost every day in peripneumonic, pleuritic and synanchic patients, and, in a word, in all those in whom something in the thorax and the lung is inflamed. This form of difficulty of breathing also follows inflammations of the liver, especially those in its convex parts, and those of the spleen, and those at its head, and those of the peritoneum.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πνευμονώδεσι, προγάστορσι

But also in those whose stomach is inflamed, and still more if its mouth and its gullet (stomachos) are, and in those in whom one of the membranes or of the muscles at the hypochondrium, or the diaphragm itself, is inflamed—in all these the breath becomes small and frequent; 853 and often too one of the small intestines, and especially those about the jejunum, and the upper parts of the colon, when inflamed to a greater degree, have compelled the person to breathe with difficulty in this way—"inflammation" (phlegmone) being understood here as the hard, hot and painful swelling, as Erasistratus is accustomed to call it and all the more recent physicians, since almost no one is unaware that the ancients used this name for the burning heat (phlogosis). It has also been shown in the book before this one that in dropsical patients and in those overfilled either with drink or with food, in pregnant women, and in the pot-bellied the breath becomes small and frequent, and, to speak generally, in all in whom any one of the respiratory organs is constricted. For thus also asthmatics and those with lung-disease (pneumonodeis) and empyics, and those whose spine is greatly distorted, and those in whom there is a formation of unconcocted tubercles either in the thorax or in the lung, and in general all in whom there is a scirrhus or some other considerable swelling of any whatsoever of all the aforementioned parts, must of necessity breathe small and frequent.

But also those whose stomach is inflamed, and the more so if it is its mouth and its stomachos, and those in whom one of the membranes or of the muscles in the hypochondrium, or the diaphragm itself, is inflamed: in all of these the breath becomes small and frequent. 853 And often also one of the thin intestines, especially those at the jejunum, and the upper parts of the colon, when more severely inflamed, has compelled the person to have difficulty in breathing in this way. For so too the asthmatic, those with affections of the lung, the empyematous, those whose spine is greatly distorted, those in whom there is a formation of unconcocted growths (phymata) either in the thorax or in the lung, and in general all who have a scirrhus or some other considerable swelling of any one at all of the parts mentioned before, must of necessity breathe small and frequent.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Neither, then, can these patients escape the notice 854 even of the ordinary run of physicians, nor can their conditions, upon which dyspnoea necessarily follows. For both the conditions are plain, and the form of the dyspnoea is evident, and it occurs in many people almost every day. But that Hippocrates writes down nothing that is so plain as this has been demonstrated many times. For Thucydides wrote the things that happened to the sick as a layman for laymen, but Hippocrates as a craftsman for craftsmen. Let us now, then, speak also of the patients themselves who suffered dyspnoea, in whose cases he did not see fit to state—not merely the form of the dyspnoea—but not even this one thing alone and simply, that they suffered dyspnoea.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐχ ὅπως

Neither, then, can these patients escape the notice 854 even of any chance physician, nor can their conditions, upon which difficulty of breathing follows of necessity. For the conditions are clear, and the form of the difficulty of breathing is evident, and it occurs in many people very nearly every day. But it has been shown many times that Hippocrates writes nothing of things so clear. For Thucydides wrote down what befell the sick as a layman for laymen, but Hippocrates as a practitioner of the art for practitioners of the art. Let us now, then, speak of the very patients who suffered difficulty of breathing, in whose case he did not see fit to state, I do not say the form of the difficulty of breathing, but not even simply this alone, that they had difficulty in breathing.

ἐπεὶ δ’ ἑπτὰ μέν ἐστιν ἅπαντα τὰ τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν βιβλία, τούτων δὲ τὸ μὲν ἕβδομον σαφῶς νόθον εἶναι πᾶσι δοκεῖ καὶ νεώτερον καὶ διεσκευασμένον, τὸ δὲ πέμπτον οὐ τοῦ μεγάλου Ἱπποκράτους τοῦ Ἡρακλείδου υἱέος, ἀλλὰ τοῦ νεωτέρου τοῦ Δράκοντος· τὸ δὲ δεύτερον καὶ τὸ τέταρτον καὶ τὸ ἕκτον ἔστι μὲν οἷς τοῦ Ἱπποκράτους υἱέος, ἔστι δὲ οἷς καὶ αὐτοῦ Ἱπποκράτους ἔδοξεν, οὐ μὴν συγγράμματά γε οὐδ’ ὥστε διαδοθῆναι τοῖς Ἕλλησι μέλλοντα, ἀλλ’ ὑπομνήματα ﻿ 855 μᾶλλον εἶναι· τισὶ δὲ οἵπερ καὶ ἀκριβέστερά μοι δοκοῦσι καταμαθεῖν τῶν βιβλίων τὴν δύναμιν, ὑπὸ μὲν τοῦ Θεσσαλοῦ γεγράφθαι δοκεῖ τὰ ε΄, δύο δ’ εἶναι τοῦ μεγάλου Ἱπποκράτους, καὶ ἐπιγεγράφθαι γέ που διὰ τοῦτο τὰ ἐκ τοῦ μικροῦ πινακιδίου, δηλονότι τοῦ Θεσσαλοῦ πάντα ὅσα περ ὁ πατὴρ αὐτοῦ γεγραφὼς ἔτυχεν ἀθροῖσαι σπουδάσαντος ἐς ταὐτὸν, ὡς μηδὲν ἀπόλοιτο· μόνα δὲ καὶ λοιπὰ τῶν ζ΄ τούτων βιβλίων Ἱπποκράτους εἶναι τοῦ μεγάλου σχεδὸν ἅπασιν ὡμολόγηται, τό τε πρῶτον καὶ τὸ τρίτον· εὔλογον ἔδοξε μοι πρῶτον μὲν τοὺς ἐν τούτοις τοῖς βιβλίοις γεγραμμένους ἀῤῥώστους προχειρίσασθαι, μετὰ δὲ τοῦτο καὶ τοὺς ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν εὐθὺς αὐτοῖς συνάπτοντα κατὰ τὴν τῶν λόγων οἰκειότητα καὶ ὅσα διὰ τῶν ἄλλων συγγραμμάτων αὐτῷ περὶ δυσπνοίας εἴρηται. καὶ δὴ καὶ πρώτου πάντων μνημονεύσωμεν Ἀναξίωνος, ὅν τινα, ὥς φησιν αὐτὸς, πυρετὸς ὀξὺς ἔλαβεν, πλευροῦ δεξιοῦ ὀδύνη ξυνεχὴς, ἔβησσε ξηρὰ, οὐδ’ ἔπτυε τὰς πρώτας ἡμέρας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διεσκευασμένον, τὰ ε΄, τὰ ἐκ τοῦ μικροῦ πινακιδίου

But since the books of the Epidemics are seven in all, and of these the seventh seems to everyone to be clearly spurious, both later and put together by revision, while the fifth is not by the great Hippocrates, the son of Heraclides, but by the younger one, the son of Draco; and the second and the fourth and the sixth have seemed to some to be by the son of Hippocrates, to others to be by Hippocrates himself, not, however, as writings, nor as intended to be circulated among the Greeks, but rather as notes 855; while to some, who indeed seem to me to have grasped the character of the books more accurately, the five seem to have been written by Thessalus, and two to be by the great Hippocrates, and for this reason, I suppose, to have been entitled "the things from the small tablet", evidently because Thessalus was eager to gather into one place everything that his father happened to have written, so that nothing should be lost; and it is agreed by almost everyone that the only remaining ones of these seven books that are by the great Hippocrates are the first and the third—it seemed reasonable to me first to take in hand the patients recorded in these books, and after that also those in the other books of the Epidemics, joining directly to them, according to the affinity of the discussions, also whatever has been said by him about difficulty of breathing in his other writings. And indeed, first of all let us mention Anaxion, whom, as he himself says, an acute fever seized; continuous pain of the right side; he coughed dry, and did not spit during the first days.
