Greek (Kühn)

τῷ δὲ μετὰ ταῦτα Ἀπολλωνίῳ τῷ ἐν Ἀβδήροις ὑποχονδρίου δεξιοῦ, φησιν, ἔπαρμα σὺν ὀδύνῃ· ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅτι διὰ παντὸς ἦν μεγαλόσπλαγχνος καὶ συνήθης αὐτῷ περὶ τὸ ὑποχόνδριον ὑπῆρχεν ὀδύνη, καὶ τοῦτ’ 887 ἐν ἀρχῇ γέγραπται τῆς διηγήσεως. οὐδὲν οὖν θαυμαστὸν, εἰ καὶ παρεφρόνησε, μὴ δυσπνοῆσαι κατὰ τὸ τῆς παραφροσύνης εἶδος αὐτὸν, οὕτως μεγάλην διάθεσιν ὀδυνηρὰν ἐν ὑποχονδρίῳ κεκτημένον. ἑξῆς δὲ τούτου ἡ ἐν Κυζίκῳ τὰς διδύμους θυγατέρας τεκοῦσά ἐστιν, ὑπὲρ ἧς ὡδί πως ἔγραψε· κεφαλῆς καὶ τραχήλου βάρος μετ’ ὀδύνης, ὥστε καὶ ἡ δύσπνοια κατὰ τοῦτο. λοιποὶ δ’ ἐπὶ ταύτῃ δύο εἰσὶν ἄῤῥωστοι κατὰ τὸ τρίτον τῶν ἐπιδημιῶν, ἡ Δεάλκους γυνὴ καὶ ὁ ἐν Μελιβοίῃ νεανίσκος, ἀμφότεροι παραφρονήσαντές τε καὶ μέγα καὶ ἀραιὸν ἔχοντες τὸ πνεῦμα, περὶ ὧν ἔμπροσθεν αὐτάρκως διελέχθη. ἀλλ’ ἐπεὶ καὶ τοῦθ’ ἡμῖν τὸ βιβλίον ἱκανὸν ἤδη μέγεθος ἔχει, περὶ τῶν λοιπῶν ἁπάντων, ὅσα περὶ δυσπνοίας εἴρηται τῷ παλαιῷ, διὰ τοῦ μετὰ ταῦτα σκεψόμεθα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

As for Apollonius in Abdera, who comes after these, he says there was a swelling of the right hypochondrium with pain; but also that he was throughout large in the viscera and that pain about the hypochondrium was habitual for him, and this too 887 has been written at the beginning of the account. It is therefore nothing to wonder at if, even though he became delirious, he did not breathe with difficulty according to the form proper to delirium, possessing as he did so great a painful condition in the hypochondrium. Next after him is the woman in Cyzicus who gave birth to the twin daughters, about whom he wrote somewhat as follows: heaviness of the head and neck with pain, so that the dyspnoea too was in accordance with this. There remain after her two patients in the third book of the Epidemics, the wife of Dealces and the young man in Meliboea, both of whom became delirious and had large and rare breathing, about whom sufficient has been said earlier. But since this book of ours already has adequate size, we shall examine in the one following this all the remaining things that have been said by the ancient about dyspnoea.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μεγαλόσπλαγχνος, διάθεσιν, κατὰ τοῦτο

Next after these, in the case of Apollonius in Abdera, there was, he says, 'a swelling of the right hypochondrium with pain'; and moreover, that he had at all times had enlarged viscera and that a pain about the hypochondrium was habitual to him, this too is written at the 887 beginning of the account. It is therefore nothing to wonder at if, even though he became deranged, he did not have difficulty of breathing according to the form proper to derangement, since he had acquired so great a painful condition in the hypochondrium. Next after him comes the woman in Cyzicus who gave birth to twin daughters, about whom he wrote somewhat as follows: 'heaviness of the head and neck with pain', so that the difficulty of breathing too was in accordance with this. After her there remain two patients in the third book of the Epidemics, the wife of Dealces and the young man in Meliboea, both of whom became deranged and had their breath large and rare; these were discussed sufficiently earlier. But since this book of ours has already reached a sufficient size, we shall examine in the next one all the remaining things that were said about difficulty of breathing by the ancient.

888 ΓΑΛΗΝΟΥ ΠΕΡΙ ΔΥΣΠΝΟΙΑς ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ ΤΡΙΤΟΝ. Τὸν περὶ τῆς δυσπνοίας λόγον εἰς δύο διδασκαλίας τεμόντες, ἐν μὲν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ὅσα χρὴ γινώσκειν ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ τὸν ἰατρὸν, ἅμα ταῖς οἰκείαις ἀποδείξεσιν ἐδηλώσαμεν· ἐν δὲ τῷ πρὸ τούτου δευτέρῳ καὶ αὐτῷ δὲ τούτῳ τῷ τρίτῳ τῶν Ἱπποκράτει περὶ δυσπνοίας εἰρημένων ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς συγγράμμασιν ἐξήγησιν ποιούμεθα· ὥστε τοὺς μὲν αὐτῶν τῶν ἔργων τῆς τέχνης μόνων ἐφιεμένους, μηδὲν δ’, ὡς αὐτοί φασιν, πολυπραγμονεῖν ἀξιοῦντας ὑπὲρ τῶν τοῖς παλαιοῖς εἰρημένων, ἔχειν ἅπαν ἱκανῶς, ὃ σπουδάζουσιν, ἐκ τοῦ πρώτου λόγου· τοὺς δὲ καὶ τὴν Ἱπποκράτους γνώμην ἐπιμαθεῖν ὀρεγομένους τε ἅμα καὶ δυναμένους, ἐκ τῶνδε τῶν 889 γραμμάτων ἀναλέγεσθαι. τὸ μὲν οὖν ὀρέγεσθαι γνῶναι τὰ τοῖς ἀρίστοις εἰρημένα, κοινὸν ἁπάντων ἐστὶ τῶν φύσει φιλοκάλων· τὸ δὲ καὶ δυνηθῆναι μαθεῖν αὐτὰ, μακροτέρου χρόνου δεόμενον, οὐκέθ’ ὁμοίως πᾶσιν ὑπάρχει· πολλοὶ γὰρ ὑπὸ πολλῆς περιστάσεως πραγμάτων ἀποστεροῦνται τῆς περὶ τὰ κάλλιστα σχολῆς. ταῦτ’ ἄρα καὶ ἡμεῖς οἴδαμεν καὶ τὸ κατεπεῖγον εἰς τὰ τῆς τέχνης ἔργα διήλθομεν ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ἰδίᾳ δ’ ἑξῆς τὴν Ἱπποκράτους γνώμην ἐξηγούμεθα διὰ τῶν ὑπολοίπων δυοῖν γραμμάτων, ὧν τὸ μὲν δεύτερόν ἐστι, τὸ δὲ τρίτον γινόμενον κατὰ τὴν ἐξ ἀρχῆς τάξιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

888 GALEN'S ON DIFFICULTY OF BREATHING, BOOK THREE. Having divided the discourse on difficulty of breathing into two courses of teaching, in the first of the commentaries we set forth what the physician must know about it, together with the demonstrations proper to it; while in the second, which precedes this one, and in this third itself we are making an exposition of what has been said by Hippocrates about difficulty of breathing in all his writings; so that those who aim only at the works of the art themselves, and who do not think it right, as they themselves put it, to busy themselves at all about what has been said by the ancients, have sufficiently from the first discourse everything they are eager for; whereas those who both desire and are able to learn in addition the opinion of Hippocrates may gather it from these 889 writings. Now the desire to know what has been said by the best men is common to all who are by nature lovers of what is fine; but also to be able to learn these things, which requires a longer time, no longer belongs to all alike; for many are deprived, by a great press of affairs, of the leisure for the finest things. Knowing this, then, we too went through what is urgent for the works of the art in the first of these commentaries, and separately, in sequence, we expound the opinion of Hippocrates through the remaining two writings, of which the one is the second and the other the third, according to the order from the beginning.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταῦτ’ ἄρα καὶ ἡμεῖς οἴδαμεν (finite οἴδαμεν where a participle such as εἰδότες would be expected; perhaps corrupt), ἐπιμαθεῖν, περιστάσεως

888 GALEN, ON DIFFICULTY OF BREATHING, BOOK THREE. Having divided the account of difficulty of breathing into two courses of instruction, in the first of the books we made clear everything that the physician must know about it, together with the proper demonstrations; in the second, which precedes this one, and in this third book itself, we are giving an exegesis of what has been said by Hippocrates about difficulty of breathing in all his treatises. The purpose is this: those who aim only at the works of the art themselves, and who do not think it worth, as they themselves put it, busying themselves at all about what was said by the ancients, may have everything they are eager for sufficiently from the first book; while those who both desire and are able to learn in addition the opinion of Hippocrates may read it in these 889 writings. Now, to desire to know what has been said by the best men is common to all who are by nature lovers of the beautiful; but actually being able to learn it, since that requires a longer time, no longer belongs to all alike; for many, through a great press of affairs, are deprived of leisure for the finest things. Knowing this, then, we on our part went through what is urgent for the works of the art in the first of these books, and next, separately, we are expounding the opinion of Hippocrates in the two remaining books, of which one is the second and the other the third according to the order from the beginning.
